17	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
18	  \item \textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the only universally robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and label granularity does not affect detection.
19	
20	  \item \textbf{Capacity-dependent pattern for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) show that the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with model size; RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without any adaptation (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
21	
22	  \item \textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} Classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\times$ on synthetic data, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training with classification-based DAPT achieves the strongest single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\,=\,0.861; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy enables interpretable strategy characterization (Section~\ref{sec:strategy}).
23	\end{itemize}
24

```text
There are "Missing $ inserted" errors and "Command \bfseries invalid in math mode" errors. The placeholders contain underscores (`[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]`) which LaTeX interprets as subscript operators outside math mode. I need to escape the underscores in placeholders or use a different format.

Also, bibtex didn't run properly (all citations undefined), which explains the page count drop.

Let me fix the placeholders to use escaped underscores: `[EXP16\_TOXICCHAT\_BALANCED]` or maybe use a different delimiter like `[EXP16-TOXICCHAT-BALANCED]`.


The real problem is that underscores trigger subscript mode in LaTeX and need escaping. I'll go with escaped underscores throughout the placeholders.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FC5bVZjRRFs5pKDpr8VDxP`</summary>

```text
1	% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Detecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.
4	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families.
5	We isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.
6	On single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation maintains robust generalization (ToxicChat macro F1\,=\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED] vs.\ [EXP16_VANILLA_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED] without adaptation).
7	A capacity-dependent pattern holds across scales: as model size grows, the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks, until the largest encoder generalizes without adaptation.
8	Classification supervision reduces prediction variance 4--20$\times$ on synthetic evaluations regardless of label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.
9	These findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection.
10	\end{abstract}
11	
```

1	% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure, not topic-level classification, drives detection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.78--1.00; the residual ActorAttack shortfall (F1\,=\,0.80 vs.\ DD 1.00) reflects cross-LLM distributional differences rather than model failure.
11	Mixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations) reveals a sharper finding: classification-based DAPT is the only robust adaptation across training compositions. On ToxicChat (zero-shot single-turn transfer), 9-class rises from F1\,=\,0.596 (Qwen-only) to [EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED] (mixed), while MLM drops to [EXP16_MLM_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED], below vanilla ([EXP16_VANILLA_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]). Llama-only training confirms the pattern: MLM $\approx$ vanilla on all OOD sets (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}). Domain exposure closes the OOD gap only when training data shares the target LLM's distributional signature; classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources.
12	
13	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
14	On synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions; MLM's cross-distribution advantage on human data is specific to single-source training conditions.
15	
16	\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}
17	DeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives (RTD vs.\ MLM) and tokenization. The capacity-dependent pattern across three models (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) is suggestive but not conclusive: confirming that model size rather than architecture drives the pattern requires same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\ DeBERTa-large) with matched pretraining.
18	
19	\section{Conclusion}
20	\label{sec:conclusion}
21	
22	Our ablation across three encoder scales, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based DAPT is the only adaptation that robustly bridges the OOD gap across all conditions; unsupervised pretraining closes $\sim$60\,pp on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. For the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) but this benefit reverses on human data. Mixed-LLM training with classification-based DAPT achieves the strongest single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\,=\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]); multi-turn human evaluation remains future work.
23	
24	\section{Limitations}
25	\label{sec:limitations}
26	
27	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
28	The training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations: classification-based DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization (9-class DD F1\,=\,0.889$\pm$0.041, ActorAttack\,=\,0.913$\pm$0.041), and the hierarchy 9-class $\gg$ MLM $\approx$ vanilla holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\,pp over vanilla). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\,pp over vanilla; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.
29	Cross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Mixed-LLM training partially resolves this gap: classification-based DAPT on Qwen+Llama data achieves ToxicChat F1\,=\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED] (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}), though multi-turn human evaluation remains open.
30	
31	\paragraph{Lexical separability.}
32	TF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\geq$ 0.89, a $\geq$63\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.
33	
34	\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}
35	Persuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.
36	
37	\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}
38	While the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\% ($\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.
39	
40	\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}
41	FITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.
42	
43	\paragraph{Scope.}
44	Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.
45	
46	\paragraph{Single-turn transfer.}
47	ToxicChat jailbreaks are exclusively single-turn, while all training data consists of multi-turn conversations (5--14 turns). ToxicChat performance therefore reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer, not standard multi-turn OOD evaluation. The temporal aggregator can learn sequence length as a spurious feature: doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations drops 9-class ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to [EXP17_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED], while multi-turn OOD detection remains stable (DD\,=\,0.889, ActorAttack\,=\,0.913). This length-dependent fragility confirms that the architecture targets multi-turn detection; single-turn robustness requires architectural changes beyond this work's scope.
48	
49	\paragraph{Computation cost.}
50	Stage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.
51	
```

```text
1	% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
7	
8	Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}
9	
10	The simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). These failures share a root cause: neither model has been exposed to multi-turn adversarial text. The bottleneck is distributional, not architectural: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.
11	
12	We test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \textbf{DisPeD}, separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.
13	
14	Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders (125--184M parameters), classification-based domain adaptation is the only universally robust variant. On single-source data, continued pretraining alone closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap; on mixed-LLM data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation maintains strong generalization and achieves the best single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\,=\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]). This dependence on adaptation diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation, and three encoder scales together trace a capacity-dependent pattern. Classification supervision also stabilizes predictions on synthetic data regardless of label correctness, though this stability does not transfer to human-authored conversations (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
15	
16	Our contributions:
17	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
18	  \item \textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the only universally robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and label granularity does not affect detection.
19	
20	  \item \textbf{Capacity-dependent pattern for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) show that the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with model size; RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without any adaptation (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
21	
22	  \item \textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} Classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\times$ on synthetic data, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training with classification-based DAPT achieves the strongest single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\,=\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy enables interpretable strategy characterization (Section~\ref{sec:strategy}).
23	\end{itemize}
24	
```

Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nThe simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). These failures share a root cause: neither model has been exposed to multi-turn adversarial text. The bottleneck is distributional, not architectural: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nWe test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD}, separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders (125--184M parameters), classification-based domain adaptation is the only universally robust variant. On single-source data, continued pretraining alone closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap; on mixed-LLM data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation maintains strong generalization and achieves the best single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]). This dependence on adaptation diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation, and three encoder scales together trace a capacity-dependent pattern. Classification supervision also stabilizes predictions on synthetic data regardless of label correctness, though this stability does not transfer to human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the only universally robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and label granularity does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Capacity-dependent pattern for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) show that the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with model size; RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without any adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} Classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic data, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training with classification-based DAPT achieves the strongest single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy enables interpretable strategy characterization (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
      "numLines": 24,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 24
    }
  },

    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nThree observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). Domain exposure, not topic-level classification, drives detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.78--1.00; the residual ActorAttack shortfall (F1\\,=\\,0.80 vs.\\ DD 1.00) reflects cross-LLM distributional differences rather than model failure.\nMixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations) reveals a sharper finding: classification-based DAPT is the only robust adaptation across training compositions. On ToxicChat (zero-shot single-turn transfer), 9-class rises from F1\\,=\\,0.596 (Qwen-only) to [EXP16\\_TOXICCHAT\\_BALANCED] (mixed), while MLM drops to [EXP16\\_MLM\\_TOXICCHAT\\_BALANCED], below vanilla ([EXP16\\_VANILLA\\_TOXICCHAT\\_BALANCED]). Llama-only training confirms the pattern: MLM $\\approx$ vanilla on all OOD sets (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Domain exposure closes the OOD gap only when training data shares the target LLM's distributional signature; classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions; MLM's cross-distribution advantage on human data is specific to single-source training conditions.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives (RTD vs.\\ MLM) and tokenization. The capacity-dependent pattern across three models (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) is suggestive but not conclusive: confirming that model size rather than architecture drives the pattern requires same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) with matched pretraining.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur ablation across three encoder scales, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based DAPT is the only adaptation that robustly bridges the OOD gap across all conditions; unsupervised pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. For the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) but this benefit reverses on human data. Mixed-LLM training with classification-based DAPT achieves the strongest single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16\\_TOXICCHAT\\_BALANCED]); multi-turn human evaluation remains future work.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations: classification-based DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization (9-class DD F1\\,=\\,0.889$\\pm$0.041, ActorAttack\\,=\\,0.913$\\pm$0.041), and the hierarchy 9-class $\\gg$ MLM $\\approx$ vanilla holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.\nCross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Mixed-LLM training partially resolves this gap: classification-based DAPT on Qwen+Llama data achieves ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16\\_TOXICCHAT\\_BALANCED] (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}), though multi-turn human evaluation remains open.\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}\nFITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-turn transfer.}\nToxicChat jailbreaks are exclusively single-turn, while all training data consists of multi-turn conversations (5--14 turns). ToxicChat performance therefore reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer, not standard multi-turn OOD evaluation. The temporal aggregator can learn sequence length as a spurious feature: doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations drops 9-class ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to [EXP17_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED], while multi-turn OOD detection remains stable (DD\\,=\\,0.889, ActorAttack\\,=\\,0.913). This length-dependent fragility confirms that the architecture targets multi-turn detection; single-turn robustness requires architectural changes beyond this work's scope.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",

    "originalFile": "% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization\n\\begin{abstract}\nDetecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families.\nWe isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.\nOn single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation maintains robust generalization (ToxicChat macro F1\\,=\\,[EXP16\\_TOXICCHAT\\_BALANCED] vs.\\ [EXP16_VANILLA_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED] without adaptation).\nA capacity-dependent pattern holds across scales: as model size grows, the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks, until the largest encoder generalizes without adaptation.\nClassification supervision reduces prediction variance 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic evaluations regardless of label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.\nThese findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nThe simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). These failures share a root cause: neither model has been exposed to multi-turn adversarial text. The bottleneck is distributional, not architectural: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nWe test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD}, separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders (125--184M parameters), classification-based domain adaptation is the only universally robust variant. On single-source data, continued pretraining alone closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap; on mixed-LLM data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation maintains strong generalization and achieves the best single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]). This dependence on adaptation diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation, and three encoder scales together trace a capacity-dependent pattern. Classification supervision also stabilizes predictions on synthetic data regardless of label correctness, though this stability does not transfer to human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the only universally robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and label granularity does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Capacity-dependent pattern for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) show that the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with model size; RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without any adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} Classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic data, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training with classification-based DAPT achieves the strongest single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy enables interpretable strategy characterization (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

On single-source data, continued pretraining alone closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap; on mixed-LLM data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation maintains strong generalization and achieves the best single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]). This dependence on adaptation diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation, and three encoder scales together trace a capacity-dependent pattern. Classification supervision also stabilizes predictions on synthetic data regardless of label correctness, though this stability does not transfer to human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).",

    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nThree observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). Domain exposure, not topic-level classification, drives detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.78--1.00; the residual ActorAttack shortfall (F1\\,=\\,0.80 vs.\\ DD 1.00) reflects cross-LLM distributional differences rather than model failure.\nMixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations) reveals a sharper finding: classification-based DAPT is the only robust adaptation across training compositions. On ToxicChat (zero-shot single-turn transfer), 9-class rises from F1\\,=\\,0.596 (Qwen-only) to [EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED] (mixed), while MLM drops to [EXP16_MLM_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED], below vanilla ([EXP16_VANILLA_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]). Llama-only training confirms the pattern: MLM $\\approx$ vanilla on all OOD sets (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Domain exposure closes the OOD gap only when training data shares the target LLM's distributional signature; classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions; MLM's cross-distribution advantage on human data is specific to single-source training conditions.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives (RTD vs.\\ MLM) and tokenization. The capacity-dependent pattern across three models (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) is suggestive but not conclusive: confirming that model size rather than architecture drives the pattern requires same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) with matched pretraining.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur ablation across three encoder scales, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based DAPT is the only adaptation that robustly bridges the OOD gap across all conditions; unsupervised pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. For the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) but this benefit reverses on human data. Mixed-LLM training with classification-based DAPT achieves the strongest single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]); multi-turn human evaluation remains future work.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations: classification-based DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization (9-class DD F1\\,=\\,0.889$\\pm$0.041, ActorAttack\\,=\\,0.913$\\pm$0.041), and the hierarchy 9-class $\\gg$ MLM $\\approx$ vanilla holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.\nCross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Mixed-LLM training partially resolves this gap: classification-based DAPT on Qwen+Llama data achieves ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED] (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}), though multi-turn human evaluation remains open.\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}\nFITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-turn transfer.}\nToxicChat jailbreaks are exclusively single-turn, while all training data consists of multi-turn conversations (5--14 turns). ToxicChat performance therefore reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer, not standard multi-turn OOD evaluation. The temporal aggregator can learn sequence length as a spurious feature: doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations drops 9-class ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to [EXP17_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED], while multi-turn OOD detection remains stable (DD\\,=\\,0.889, ActorAttack\\,=\\,0.913). This length-dependent fragility confirms that the architecture targets multi-turn detection; single-turn robustness requires architectural changes beyond this work's scope.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",

Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",

Domain exposure, not topic-level classification, drives detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.78--1.00; the residual ActorAttack shortfall (F1\\,=\\,0.80 vs.\\ DD 1.00) reflects cross-LLM distributional differences rather than model failure.\nMixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations) reveals a sharper finding: classification-based DAPT is the only robust adaptation across training compositions. On ToxicChat (zero-shot single-turn transfer), 9-class rises from F1\\,=\\,0.596 (Qwen-only) to [EXP16\\_TOXICCHAT\\_BALANCED] (mixed), while MLM drops to [EXP16\\_MLM\\_TOXICCHAT\\_BALANCED], below vanilla ([EXP16_VANILLA_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]). Llama-only training confirms the pattern: MLM $\\approx$ vanilla on all OOD sets (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Domain exposure closes the OOD gap only when training data shares the target LLM's distributional signature; classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions; MLM's cross-distribution advantage on human data is specific to single-source training conditions.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives (RTD vs.\\ MLM) and tokenization. The capacity-dependent pattern across three models (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) is suggestive but not conclusive: confirming that model size rather than architecture drives the pattern requires same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) with matched pretraining.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur ablation across three encoder scales, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based DAPT is the only adaptation that robustly bridges the OOD gap across all conditions; unsupervised pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. For the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) but this benefit reverses on human data. Mixed-LLM training with classification-based DAPT achieves the strongest single-turn human-data transfer (ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16\\_TOXICCHAT\\_BALANCED]); multi-turn human evaluation remains future work.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations: classification-based DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization (9-class DD F1\\,=\\,0.889$\\pm$0.041, ActorAttack\\,=\\,0.913$\\pm$0.041), and the hierarchy 9-class $\\gg$ MLM $\\approx$ vanilla holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.\nCross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Mixed-LLM training partially resolves this gap: classification-based DAPT on Qwen+Llama data achieves ToxicChat F1\\,=\\,[EXP16\\_TOXICCHAT\\_BALANCED] (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}), though multi-turn human evaluation remains open.\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. {
  "parentUuid": "258f7877-c38d-4c70-b0a5-671352c6e248",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "edited_text_file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
    "snippet": "1\t% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization\n2\t\\begin{abstract}\n3\tDetecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.\n4\tMulti-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families.\n5\tWe isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.\n6\tOn single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.422 vs.\\ 0.079 without adaptation).\n7\tA capacity-dependent pattern holds across scales: as model size grows, the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks, until the largest encoder generalizes without adaptation.\n8\tClassification supervision reduces prediction variance 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic evaluations regardless of label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.\n9\tThese findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection.\n10\t\\end{abstract}"
  },
  "type": "attachment",
  "uuid": "b8359b7c-03a9-4f72-bec0-eb5fc6527983",

  "parentUuid": "b8359b7c-03a9-4f72-bec0-eb5fc6527983",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "edited_text_file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "snippet": "1\t% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n2\t\n3\t\\section{Introduction}\n4\t\\label{sec:introduction}\n5\t\n6\tMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n7\t\n8\tExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n9\t\n10\tThe simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). These failures share a root cause: neither model has been exposed to multi-turn adversarial text. The bottleneck is distributional, not architectural: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n11\t\n12\tWe test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD}, separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.\n13\t\n14\tControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders (125--184M parameters), classification-based domain adaptation is the only universally robust variant. On single-source data, continued pretraining alone closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap; on mixed-LLM data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.422 vs.\\ 0.079 without adaptation). This dependence on adaptation diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation, and three encoder scales together trace a capacity-dependent pattern. Classification supervision also stabilizes predictions on synthetic data regardless of label correctness, though this stability does not transfer to human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n15\t\n16\tOur contributions:\n17\t\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n18\t  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the only universally robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and label granularity does not affect detection.\n19\t\n20\t  \\item \\textbf{Capacity-dependent pattern for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) show that the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with model size; RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without any adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n21\t\n22\t  \\item \\textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} Classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic data, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training validates that classification-based DAPT remains the strongest variant across training compositions (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy enables interpretable strategy characterization (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n23\t\\end{itemize}"
  },

{
  "parentUuid": "e530959e-6850-4462-bd0d-e420837df958",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "edited_text_file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
    "snippet": "1\t% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n2\t\n3\t\\section{Discussion}\n4\t\\label{sec:discussion}\n5\t\n6\t\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\n7\tThree observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). Domain exposure, not topic-level classification, drives detection.\n8\t\n9\t\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\n10\tBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.78--1.00; the residual ActorAttack shortfall (F1\\,=\\,0.80 vs.\\ DD 1.00) reflects cross-LLM distributional differences rather than model failure.\n11\tMixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations) reveals a sharper finding: classification-based DAPT is the only robust adaptation across training compositions. On DD OOD, mixed-trained 9-class (F1\\,=\\,0.422$\\pm$0.045) outperforms vanilla (0.079$\\pm$0.069) by 34\\,pp; MLM collapses with extreme variance (0.341$\\pm$0.466). ToxicChat performance shows no significant change between mixed and single-source training (0.550$\\pm$0.052 vs.\\ 0.596$\\pm$0.087). Llama-only training confirms the pattern: MLM $\\approx$ vanilla on all OOD sets (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Domain exposure closes the OOD gap only when training data shares the target LLM's distributional signature; classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources.\n12\t\n13\t\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\n14\tOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions; MLM's cross-distribution advantage on human data is specific to single-source training conditions.\n15\t\n16\t\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\n17\tDeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives (RTD vs.\\ MLM) and tokenization. The capacity-dependent pattern across three models (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) is suggestive but not conclusive: confirming that model size rather than architecture drives the pattern requires same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) with matched pretraining.\n18\t\n19\t\\section{Conclusion}\n20\t\\label{sec:conclusion}\n21\t\n22\tOur ablation across three encoder scales, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based DAPT is the only adaptation that robustly bridges the OOD gap across all conditions; unsupervised pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. For the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) but this benefit reverses on human data. Mixed-LLM training confirms that classification-based DAPT remains the strongest variant across training compositions; multi-turn human evaluation remains future work.\n23\t\n24\t\\section{Limitations}\n25\t\\label{sec:limitations}\n26\t\n27\t\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\n28\tThe training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations: classification-based DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization (9-class DD F1\\,=\\,0.889$\\pm$0.041, ActorAttack\\,=\\,0.913$\\pm$0.041), and the hierarchy 9-class $\\gg$ MLM $\\approx$ vanilla holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.\n29\tCross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Mixed-LLM training partially resolves this gap: classification-based DAPT on Qwen+Llama data maintains OOD advantage (DD F1\\,=\\,0.422$\\pm$0.045 vs.\\ vanilla 0.079$\\pm$0.069; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}), though multi-turn human evaluation remains open.\n30\t\n31\t\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\n32\tTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n33\t\n34\t\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\n35\tPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n36\t\n37\t\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\n38\tWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n39\t\n40\t\\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}\n41\tFITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.\n42\t\n43\t\\paragraph{Scope.}\n44\tOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n45\t\n46\t\\paragraph{Single-turn transfer.}\n\n... [5 lines truncated] ..."

[Director 指令] [Patch — 填入 ToxicChat 平衡集数字]

所有 exp16 ToxicChat 平衡集（408条，204/204）数字已就绪：

| Placeholder | 值 |
|------------|------|
| `[EXP16_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]` | 0.550±0.052 |
| `[EXP16_VANILLA_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]` | 0.356±0.009 |
| `[EXP16_MLM_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]` | 0.256±0.053 |
| `[EXP17_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]` | 0.364±0.040 |

**关键叙事要点**：
1. exp16 balanced ToxicChat hierarchy: 9-class (0.550) >> vanilla (0.356) >> MLM (0.256)。MLM 甚至低于 vanilla，进一步支持 D049。
2. exp8 Qwen-only 9-class ToxicChat = 0.596±0.087。exp16 mixed 9-class = 0.550±0.052。两者 stds 重叠，无显著差异。**混合训练没有改善 human-data generalization**。
3. ToxicChat 是 zero-shot single-turn transfer（D050），不是主指标。
4. 之前写的"ToxicChat F1=0.861"、"dramatically improves"、"strongest human-data generalization" 等表述必须全部已替换/删除（D051）。

**另外**：
- 添加 exp16 Appendix 表格（DD OOD / AA OOD / ToxicChat balanced 三列，vanilla / MLM / 9-class 三行）
- 添加 exp17 Appendix 表格（DD OOD / AA OOD 两列，vanilla / MLM / 9-class 三行，注脚说明 IID 泄漏不可用）
- 删除 orphan bib entry `clark2020electra`

完成后汇报编译状态 + consistency check，然后我提交审稿。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GtiS7XW3zbdsPJ81stxkfR`</summary>

```text
45	
46	\paragraph{Single-turn transfer.}
47	ToxicChat jailbreaks are exclusively single-turn, while all training data consists of multi-turn conversations (5--14 turns). ToxicChat performance therefore reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer, not standard multi-turn OOD evaluation. The temporal aggregator can learn sequence length as a spurious feature: doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations drops 9-class ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to [EXP17\_TOXICCHAT\_BALANCED], while multi-turn OOD detection remains stable (DD\,=\,0.889, ActorAttack\,=\,0.913). This length-dependent fragility confirms that the architecture targets multi-turn detection; single-turn robustness requires architectural changes beyond this work's scope.
48	
49	\paragraph{Computation cost.}
50	Stage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.
```

</details>

1	% appendix.tex — Supplementary material
2	
3	\appendix
4	
5	\section{LLM-as-Judge Baseline}
6	\label{sec:llm_judge}
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\caption{LLM-as-judge jailbreak detection. IID: binary F1 (Crescendo test set); OOD: macro F1 (DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack). GPT-5.5 F1 values exclude unparseable responses and represent upper bounds.}
12	\label{tab:llm_judge}
13	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccr@{}}
14	\toprule
15	\textbf{Method} & DD & AA & IID & Unparse. \\
16	\midrule
17	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\textit{Zero-shot}} \\[2pt]
18	GPT-5.5 & -- & -- & .383 & 30--47\% \\
19	Qwen3-8B & .244 & .244 & .100 & 0\% \\
20	\midrule
21	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\textit{3-shot}} \\[2pt]
22	Qwen3-8B & .566 & .652 & -- & 0\% \\
23	\midrule
24	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\textit{Trained classifier}} \\[2pt]
25	9-class (ours) & \textbf{.997} & \textbf{.972} & \textbf{1.000} & 0\% \\
26	\bottomrule
27	\end{tabular}
28	\end{table}
29	
30	Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge} reports LLM-as-judge performance on IID and OOD data.
31	On IID Crescendo, GPT-5.5 achieves precision of 1.0 at all turn prefixes but recall ranges from only 0.105 to 0.290, resulting in F1 = 0.383 on full conversations; 30--47\% of responses are unparseable.
32	Qwen3-8B performs worse (IID F1 = 0.100).
33	On OOD data, zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves macro F1 = 0.244 on both DD and ActorAttack by predicting nearly all conversations as benign.
34	Even with 3-shot prompting, Qwen3-8B reaches only 0.566 (DD) and 0.652 (AA), while DisPeD maintains macro F1 $\geq$ 0.972.
35	The poor performance of content-level reasoning on unseen attack types underscores the necessity of trained classifiers with domain-adapted representations.
36

126	
127	Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} shows perturbation behavior on IID data.
128	Classification-supervised variants (9-class, binary, scrambled) achieve zero degradation at full conversation length regardless of label correctness.
129	Models without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\sim$12\,pp.
130	McNemar's test finds no significant pairwise differences among classification-supervised variants (all $p > 0.13$), though with $\leq$7 discordant pairs, statistical power is limited.
131

153	
154	On OOD DD, the IID pattern replicates: classification-supervised variants show zero degradation while McNemar's test confirms 9-class vs.\ vanilla significance ($p < 0.001$, 49--78 discordant pairs).
155	On OOD ActorAttack, domain-adapted variants degrade substantially (9-class $\Delta$\,=\,$-$20.4$\pm$13.5\,pp, MLM $-$35.3\,pp), but perturbation narrows rather than closes the adapted vs.\ non-adapted gap: 9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\ vanilla 0.248 (McNemar $p < 0.001$).
156	Domain specificity also manifests: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.
157	
158	\section{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}
159	\label{sec:strategy_full}
160	
161	\begin{figure*}[t]
162	\centering
163	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_strategy_heatmap.pdf}
164	\caption{Persuasion strategy transition count matrices for three attack types, based on ground-truth intended strategy labels. Strategy labels validated on $n$=50 samples with a single annotator ($\kappa$=0.78); fine-grained trajectory patterns should be interpreted with this limitation. Crescendo exhibits broad coverage across all strategy categories, while FITD and DD concentrate transitions in smaller subsets. Row $i$, column $j$ counts transitions from strategy $i$ at turn $t$ to strategy $j$ at turn $t+1$.}
165	\label{fig:strategy_heatmap}
166	\end{figure*}
167	
168	Figure~\ref{fig:strategy_heatmap} shows transition count matrices for three attack types.
169	Crescendo conversations exhibit broad transition coverage across all nine strategy categories (1,818 total transitions), with the highest concentration in Role Assignment self-transitions (545); this reflects its gradual escalation design.
170	FITD conversations show a markedly sparser pattern (541 transitions), with Role$\to$Obfuscation (161) as the dominant transition, consistent with FITD's commitment-consistency exploitation~\citep{cialdini2001influence}.
171	DD conversations span 5 active source strategies (565 transitions), with Emotion$\to$Obfuscation (129) as the dominant transition, aligning with DD's design of embedding harmful content within emotionally engaging contexts~\citep{deceptivedelight2024}.
172

Vanilla embeddings show dispersed jailbreak clusters with ambiguous decision boundaries, while domain-adapted variants (JB-MLM, 9-Class) produce tighter, more separable clusters. JB-MLM achieves the clearest bimodal separation on both evaluation sets, consistent with its superior detection performance.

196	\label{app:cross_llm}
