199	\centering
200	\footnotesize
201	\caption{Cross-LLM validation (F1, full conversation). All models are trained on Qwen3-8B data; conditions vary the source of jailbreak (JB) and benign (ben.) conversations at inference. Values are evaluated under a matched 50-sample subset protocol and differ from Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}, which uses the full evaluation set.}
202	\label{tab:cross_llm}
203	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}
204	\toprule
205	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textit{DD Full}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textit{AA Full}} \\
206	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7}
207	\textbf{Condition} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\
208	\midrule
209	Qwen JB + Qwen ben. & .312 & .914 & .997 & .258 & .893 & .972 \\
210	Llama JB + Llama ben. & .261 & .282 & .433 & .261 & .368 & .321 \\
211	Llama JB + Qwen ben. & .302 & .778$^{*}$ & 1.000 & .302 & .903 & .801 \\
212	\bottomrule
213	\end{tabular}
214	\vspace{-2pt}
215	{\footnotesize $^{*}$High seed variance (std = 0.306): seed 456 outlier (F1 = 0.345); remaining two seeds achieve F1 $\geq$ 0.97.}
216	\end{table}
217	
218	Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm} shows three inference conditions. When both jailbreak and benign text come from Llama (row~2), all variants collapse (F1 = 0.26--0.43), but this conflates two sources of distribution shift: unfamiliar jailbreak patterns and unfamiliar benign text. Row~3 isolates jailbreak transferability by pairing Llama jailbreaks with the original Qwen benign distribution. Under this condition, 9-class recovers to F1 = 1.000 on DD and 0.801 on ActorAttack, and JB-MLM reaches 0.903 on ActorAttack. The contrast between rows~2 and~3 shows that the dominant failure mode in row~2 is benign distribution mismatch (false positives from unfamiliar benign text), not inability to recognize cross-LLM jailbreak patterns. Vanilla remains at 0.26--0.31 across all conditions, further confirming that encoders without DAPT lack OOD detection capability regardless of the LLM source. The residual degradation on ActorAttack (0.801 vs.\ 0.972) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation in structurally complex attack patterns.
219	
220	\begin{table}[ht]
221	\centering
222	\footnotesize
223	\caption{Error decomposition for DD Full cross-LLM conditions (matched 50-sample subsets). TP rate is identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse in the Llama-only condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.}
224	\label{tab:cross_llm_error}
225	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
226	\toprule
227	\textbf{Metric} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\
228	\midrule
229	TP rate (Llama JB) & 0.0\% & 68.0\% & 100.0\% \\
230	FP rate (Llama ben.) & 29.3\% & 88.0\% & 90.0\% \\
231	FP rate (Qwen ben.) & 0.0\% & 0.9\% & 0.0\% \\
232	\bottomrule
233	\end{tabular}
234	\end{table}
235	
236	Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error} decomposes DD Full F1 into true positive and false positive rates. TP rates are identical regardless of the benign source: domain-adapted models detect Llama-generated jailbreaks at the same rate whether evaluated against Qwen or Llama benign text. The F1 collapse in the full Llama condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (FP rate: 88--90\% on Llama vs.\ 0--0.9\% on Qwen for adapted variants). The model implicitly treats non-Qwen text style as a jailbreak indicator, a spurious correlation from single-LLM training data. This failure reflects a data diversity limitation, not a methodological one; multi-LLM training data would address this confound directly.
237	
238	\section{RoBERTa-large DAPT Learning Rate Control}
239	\label{app:roberta_large_lr}
240	
241	\begin{table}[ht]
242	\centering
243	\footnotesize
244	\caption{RoBERTa-large (355M) DAPT learning rate control on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). Reducing the learning rate worsens both DD and AA, ruling out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression in Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}.}
245	\label{tab:roberta_large_lr}
246	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
247	\toprule
248	\textbf{Config} & DD Full & AA Full \\
249	\midrule
250	Vanilla (no DAPT) & .927$\pm$.036 & 1.000$\pm$.000 \\
251	9-class, LR=$2 \times 10^{-5}$ & .987$\pm$.004 & .946$\pm$.057 \\
252	9-class, LR=$1 \times 10^{-5}$ & .948$\pm$.034 & .781$\pm$.054 \\
253	\bottomrule
254	\end{tabular}
255	\end{table}
256	
257	Reducing the DAPT learning rate from $2 \times 10^{-5}$ to $1 \times 10^{-5}$ worsens both DD and AA performance, which rules out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression observed in Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}. An MLM-only variant (without classification) produces the same pattern (AA Full = 0.874$\pm$0.014); representation drift from continued pretraining, not classification-specific bias, is the underlying cause. Domain-adaptive fine-tuning narrows the representation space of a sufficiently large encoder and reduces its inherent OOD generalization ability.
258	
259	\section{Reproducibility Details}
260	\label{sec:reproducibility}
261	
262	\paragraph{Stage-1 training.}
263	All variants use DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) with learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, AdamW optimizer ($\beta_1$=0.9, $\beta_2$=0.999, weight decay 0.01), linear warmup over the first 10\% of steps, batch size 16, and 4 epochs. MLM variants use 15\% random token masking. Training takes approximately 30 minutes per variant on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB).
264	
265	\paragraph{Stage-2 training.}
266	The BiGRU has hidden dimension 256, 2 layers, and dropout 0.3 (bidirectional, so output dimension 512). The MLP classifier has layers $512 \to 256 \to 2$ with ReLU activation. Training uses learning rate $1 \times 10^{-3}$, Adam optimizer, batch size 32, and 20 epochs with early stopping (patience 5) on validation F1. Stage-2 training completes in under 5 minutes per variant.
267	
268	\paragraph{Data generation.}
269	Jailbreak conversations are generated using Qwen3-8B with attack-specific prompt templates. Crescendo conversations follow a 5--8 turn gradual escalation structure. FITD conversations use a 2--3 turn commitment-consistency structure. Deceptive Delight conversations embed harmful content within emotionally engaging 3--5 turn sequences. Benign conversations are generated with topic-anchored prompts across five domains (technology, health, finance, education, general) with matched turn counts.
270	
271	\paragraph{Strategy annotation.}
272	Each user turn is annotated by Qwen3-8B using a structured prompt that provides definitions and two examples for each of the nine strategy categories. The prompt instructs the model to output a single category label. We plan to release all generation and annotation prompts, the full dataset, and training code upon acceptance.
273	
274	\paragraph{Adversarial paraphrasing.}
275	The paraphrased test set is generated by instructing Qwen3-8B to rephrase each user turn while preserving the communicative intent. The resulting paraphrases have mean BERTScore 0.92 and mean lexical overlap 0.20 with the originals. These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.
276

(ours) & \\textbf{0.0} & \\textbf{$-$2.6} & \\textbf{0.0} & \\textbf{0.0} \\\\\nScrambled & \\textbf{0.0} & $-$5.3 & $-$2.6 & \\textbf{0.0} \\\\\nBinary & \\textbf{0.0} & $-$9.2 & $-$2.6 & limited.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Perturbation robustness on OOD Deceptive Delight ($n$=118). $\\Delta$: F1 change (pp) from clean to paraphrased. 3-seed mean$\\pm$std & .914$\\pm$.115 & .941 & +2.7 & 0.0 \\\\\nWiki MLM$^{*}$ & .904$\\pm$.115 & .872 & $-$3.3 & $-$3.2 \\\\\nVanilla$^{\\dagger}$ & .312$\\pm$.026 & .339$\\pm$.055 & +2.7 & $-$6.8$\\pm$1.1 change.\\\\$^{\\dagger}$Vanilla $\\Delta$ Full = +2.7\\,pp reflects floor-effect noise (baseline 0.312, std 0.055), not meaningful improvement.}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn OOD DD, the IID pattern replicates: classification-supervised variants show zero degradation while McNemar's test confirms 9-class vs.\\ vanilla significance ($p < 0.001$, 49--78 discordant pairs).\nOn OOD ActorAttack, domain-adapted variants degrade substantially (9-class $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$20.4$\\pm$13.5\\,pp, MLM $-$35.3\\,pp), but perturbation narrows rather than closes the adapted vs.\\ non-adapted gap: 9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\\ vanilla 0.248 (McNemar $p < 0.001$).\nDomain specificity also manifests: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\\sim$12\\,pp while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\\,pp.\n\n\\section{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}\n\\label{sec:strategy_full}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_strategy_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Persuasion strategy transition count matrices for three attack types, based on ground-truth intended strategy labels. Strategy labels validated on $n$=50 samples with a single annotator ($\\kappa$=0.78); fine-grained trajectory patterns should be interpreted with this limitation. Crescendo exhibits broad coverage across all strategy categories, while FITD and DD concentrate transitions in smaller subsets. Row $i$, column $j$ counts transitions from strategy $i$ at turn $t$ to strategy $j$ at turn $t+1$.}\n\\label{fig:strategy_heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:strategy_heatmap} shows transition count matrices for three attack types.\nCrescendo conversations exhibit broad transition coverage across all nine strategy categories (1,818 total transitions), with the highest concentration in Role Assignment self-transitions (545); this reflects its gradual escalation design.\nFITD conversations show a markedly sparser pattern (541 transitions), with Role$\\to$Obfuscation (161) as the dominant transition, consistent with FITD's commitment-consistency exploitation~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}.\nDD conversations span 5 active source strategies (565 transitions), with Emotion$\\to$Obfuscation (129) as the dominant transition, aligning with DD's design of embedding harmful content within emotionally engaging contexts~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024}.\n\n\\section{Centered CKA Heatmaps}\n\\label{sec:cka_heatmap}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/cka_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Centered CKA similarity~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} between encoder variants on IID and OOD data for DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa. Low off-diagonal values (vanilla vs.\\ adapted variants) confirm that continued pretraining substantially reshapes representations; the MLM vs.\\ 9-class block shows distinct geometries (CKA $\\leq$ 0.2 on RoBERTa OOD).}\n\\label{fig:cka_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Embedding Space Visualization}\n\\label{app:tsne}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:tsne} visualizes mean-pooled \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via t-SNE for three representative variants (Vanilla, JB-MLM, 9-Class) on DD OOD and ActorAttack. Vanilla embeddings show dispersed jailbreak clusters with ambiguous decision boundaries, while domain-adapted variants (JB-MLM, 9-Class) produce tighter, more separable clusters. JB-MLM achieves the clearest bimodal separation on both evaluation sets, consistent with its superior detection performance.\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/embedding_tsne.pdf}\n\\caption{t-SNE visualization of mean-pooled \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings (perplexity=30) for Vanilla, JB-MLM, and 9-Class variants on DD OOD and ActorAttack (118 samples each; blue=benign, red=jailbreak).}\n\\label{fig:tsne}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Cross-LLM Validation}\n\\label{app:cross_llm}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Cross-LLM validation (F1, full conversation). All models are trained on Qwen3-8B data; conditions vary the source of jailbreak (JB) and benign (ben.) conversations at inference. Values are evaluated under a matched 50-sample subset protocol and differ from Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}, which uses the full evaluation set.}\n\\label{tab:cross_llm}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textit{DD Full}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textit{AA Full}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Condition} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen JB + Qwen ben. & .312 & .914 & .997 & .258 & .893 & .972 \\\\\nLlama JB + Llama ben. & .261 & .282 & .433 & .261 & .368 & .321 \\\\\nLlama JB + Qwen ben. & .302 & .778$^{*}$ & 1.000 & .302 & .903 & .801 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^{*}$High seed variance (std = 0.306): seed 456 outlier (F1 = 0.345); remaining two seeds achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.97.}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_llm} shows three inference conditions. When both jailbreak and benign text come from Llama (row~2), all variants collapse (F1 = 0.26--0.43), but this conflates two sources of distribution shift: unfamiliar jailbreak patterns and unfamiliar benign text. Row~3 isolates jailbreak transferability by pairing Llama jailbreaks with the original Qwen benign distribution. Under this condition, 9-class recovers to F1 = 1.000 on DD and 0.801 on ActorAttack, and JB-MLM reaches 0.903 on ActorAttack. The contrast between rows~2 and~3 shows that the dominant failure mode in row~2 is benign distribution mismatch (false positives from unfamiliar benign text), not inability to recognize cross-LLM jailbreak patterns. Vanilla remains at 0.26--0.31 across all conditions, further confirming that encoders without DAPT lack OOD detection capability regardless of the LLM source. The residual degradation on ActorAttack (0.801 vs.\\ 0.972) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation in structurally complex attack patterns.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Error decomposition for DD Full cross-LLM conditions (matched 50-sample subsets). TP rate is identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse in the Llama-only condition (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.}\n\\label{tab:cross_llm_error}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTP rate (Llama JB) & 0.0\\% & 68.0\\% & 100.0\\% \\\\\nFP rate (Llama ben.) & 29.3\\% & 88.0\\% & 90.0\\% \\\\\nFP rate (Qwen ben.) & 0.0\\% & 0.9\\% & 0.0\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error} decomposes DD Full F1 into true positive and false positive rates. TP rates are identical regardless of the benign source: domain-adapted models detect Llama-generated jailbreaks at the same rate whether evaluated against Qwen or Llama benign text. The F1 collapse in the full Llama condition (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (FP rate: 88--90\\% on Llama vs.\\ 0--0.9\\% on Qwen for adapted variants). The model implicitly treats non-Qwen text style as a jailbreak indicator, a spurious correlation from single-LLM training data. This failure reflects a data diversity limitation, not a methodological one; multi-LLM training data would address this confound directly.\n\n\\section{RoBERTa-large DAPT Learning Rate Control}\n\\label{app:roberta_large_lr}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{RoBERTa-large (355M) DAPT learning rate control on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Reducing the learning rate worsens both DD and AA, ruling out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}.}\n\\label{tab:roberta_large_lr}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & DD Full & AA Full \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla (no DAPT) & .927$\\pm$.036 & 1.000$\\pm$.000 \\\\\n9-class, LR=$2 \\times 10^{-5}$ & .987$\\pm$.004 & .946$\\pm$.057 \\\\\n9-class, LR=$1 \\times 10^{-5}$ & .948$\\pm$.034 & .781$\\pm$.054 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nReducing the DAPT learning rate from $2 \\times 10^{-5}$ to $1 \\times 10^{-5}$ worsens both DD and AA performance, which rules out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression observed in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}. An MLM-only variant (without classification) produces the same pattern (AA Full = 0.874$\\pm$0.014); representation drift from continued pretraining, not classification-specific bias, is the underlying cause. Domain-adaptive fine-tuning narrows the representation space of a sufficiently large encoder and reduces its inherent OOD generalization ability.\n\n\\section{Reproducibility Details}\n\\label{sec:reproducibility}\n\n\\paragraph{Stage-1 training.}\nAll variants use DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) with learning rate $2 \\times 10^{-5}$, AdamW optimizer ($\\beta_1$=0.9, $\\beta_2$=0.999, weight decay 0.01), linear warmup over the first 10\\% of steps, batch size 16, and 4 epochs. MLM variants use 15\\% random token masking. Training takes approximately 30 minutes per variant on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB).\n\n\\paragraph{Stage-2 training.}\nThe BiGRU has hidden dimension 256, 2 layers, and dropout 0.3 (bidirectional, so output dimension 512). The MLP classifier has layers $512 \\to 256 \\to 2$ with ReLU activation. Training uses learning rate $1 \\times 10^{-3}$, Adam optimizer, batch size 32, and 20 epochs with early stopping (patience 5) on validation F1. Stage-2 training completes in under 5 minutes per variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Data generation.}\nJailbreak conversations are generated using Qwen3-8B with attack-specific prompt templates. Crescendo conversations follow a 5--8 turn gradual escalation structure. FITD conversations use a 2--3 turn commitment-consistency structure. Deceptive Delight conversations embed harmful content within emotionally engaging 3--5 turn sequences. Benign conversations are generated with topic-anchored prompts across five domains (technology, health, finance, education, general) with matched turn counts.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation.}\nEach user turn is annotated by Qwen3-8B using a structured prompt that provides definitions and two examples for each of the nine strategy categories. The prompt instructs the model to output a single category label. We plan to release all generation and annotation prompts, the full dataset, and training code upon acceptance.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial paraphrasing.}\nThe paraphrased test set is generated by instructing Qwen3-8B to rephrase each user turn while preserving the communicative intent. The resulting paraphrases have mean BERTScore 0.92 and mean lexical overlap 0.20 with the originals. These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.\n",

  "new_text": "This failure reflects a data diversity limitation, not a methodological one; mixed-LLM training (Table~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm}) partially addresses this confound.\n\n\\section{Mixed-LLM Training Results}\n\\label{app:mixed_llm}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Mixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std where available). ToxicChat evaluated on balanced set (408 conversations). MLM exhibits extreme variance on DD OOD (std\\,=\\,0.466), driven by one seed predicting all-jailbreak.}\n\\label{tab:mixed_llm}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Mixed & DD OOD & AA OOD & ToxicChat \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{.996$\\pm$.006} & \\textbf{.422$\\pm$.045} & \\textbf{.217$\\pm$.079} & \\textbf{.550$\\pm$.052} \\\\\nMLM & .681 & .341$\\pm$.466 & .146 & .256$\\pm$.053 \\\\\nVanilla & .802 & .079$\\pm$.069 & .166 & .356$\\pm$.009 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm} reports detection performance when training on a mixed corpus of Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B conversations. Classification-based DAPT (9-class) maintains the strongest performance across all evaluation conditions. MLM continued pretraining collapses on DD OOD with extreme seed variance (std\\,=\\,0.466), falling below vanilla on ToxicChat (0.256 vs.\\ 0.356). ToxicChat performance shows no significant improvement over single-source training (0.550$\\pm$0.052 vs.\\ 0.596$\\pm$0.087; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), consistent with ToxicChat being zero-shot single-turn transfer rather than a standard OOD benchmark.\n\n\\section{Dataset Scale Validation}\n\\label{app:scale_validation}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Dataset scale validation (1{,}000 Qwen conversations, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). IID results omitted due to train/test overlap (71\\% of original test set present in expanded training data).}\n\\label{tab:scale_validation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD OOD & AA OOD \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{.889$\\pm$.041} & \\textbf{.913$\\pm$.041} \\\\\nMLM & .338$\\pm$.010 & .351$\\pm$.018 \\\\\nVanilla & .326$\\pm$.000 & .508$\\pm$.085 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scale_validation} validates robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations with new topic seeds. The hierarchy 9-class $\\gg$ MLM $\\approx$ vanilla holds at 2$\\times$ scale: classification-based DAPT maintains 40--56\\,pp advantage over vanilla, while MLM provides no improvement over vanilla on either attack family (DD: 0.338 vs.\\ 0.326; AA: 0.351 vs.\\ 0.508).\n\n\\section{RoBERTa-large DAPT Learning Rate Control}",

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - EMNLP 2026 ARR submission (8-page main text, unlimited refs/limitations)
   - Domain-Adaptive Pretraining (DAPT) — MLM vs classification-based (9-class)
   - Evidence hierarchy: vanilla < MLM < scrambled ≈ binary ≈ 9-class (on single-source Qwen data only)
   - D049 reframe: MLM effectiveness is condition-dependent (Qwen-specific); 9-class is universally robust
   - Cross-LLM validation: exp16 (mixed Qwen+Llama), exp16b (Llama-only), exp17 (1000 Qwen scale)
   - ToxicChat as zero-shot single-turn transfer (D050), NOT standard OOD benchmark
   - GRU length feature: sequence length as spurious feature causing ToxicChat regression at larger scale
   - M020: ToxicChat eval set mismatch — unbalanced (242, 84:16) vs balanced (408, 50:50)

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **docs/paper/abstract.tex** (truth source, modified multiple times):
     - Final state after all patches:
     ```latex
     \begin{abstract}
     Detecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.
     Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families.
     We isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.
     On single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\,=\,0.422 vs.\ 0.079 without adaptation).
     A capacity-dependent pattern holds across scales: as model size grows, the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks, until the largest encoder generalizes without adaptation.
     Classification supervision reduces prediction variance 4--20$\times$ on synthetic evaluations regardless of label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.
     These findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection.
     \end{abstract}
     ```
     - Key changes: Removed "domain text exposure alone suffices" opener. Conditioned "60pp" with "On single-source synthetic data." Used DD OOD numbers (0.422 vs 0.079) instead of ToxicChat. Final sentence reframed to "adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure."

   - **docs/paper/introduction.tex** (modified multiple times):
     - Para 14 reframed: "classification-based domain adaptation is the only universally robust variant"

{
  "parentUuid": "ae684769-9c5a-40fe-abd0-56a3a85dd8ff",

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nThree observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). Domain exposure, not topic-level classification, drives detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.78--1.00; the residual ActorAttack shortfall (F1\\,=\\,0.80 vs.\\ DD 1.00) reflects cross-LLM distributional differences rather than model failure.\nMixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations) reveals a sharper finding: classification-based DAPT is the only robust adaptation across training compositions. On DD OOD, mixed-trained 9-class (F1\\,=\\,0.422$\\pm$0.045) outperforms vanilla (0.079$\\pm$0.069) by 34\\,pp; MLM collapses with extreme variance (0.341$\\pm$0.466). ToxicChat performance shows no significant change between mixed and single-source training (0.550$\\pm$0.052 vs.\\ 0.596$\\pm$0.087). Llama-only training confirms the pattern: MLM $\\approx$ vanilla on all OOD sets (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Domain exposure closes the OOD gap only when training data shares the target LLM's distributional signature; classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions; MLM's cross-distribution advantage on human data is specific to single-source training conditions.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives (RTD vs.\\ MLM) and tokenization. The capacity-dependent pattern across three models (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) is suggestive but not conclusive: confirming that model size rather than architecture drives the pattern requires same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) with matched pretraining.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur ablation across three encoder scales, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based DAPT is the only adaptation that robustly bridges the OOD gap across all conditions; unsupervised pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. For the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) but this benefit reverses on human data. Mixed-LLM training confirms that classification-based DAPT remains the strongest variant across training compositions; multi-turn human evaluation remains future work.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations: classification-based DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization (9-class DD F1\\,=\\,0.889$\\pm$0.041, ActorAttack\\,=\\,0.913$\\pm$0.041), and the hierarchy 9-class $\\gg$ MLM $\\approx$ vanilla holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.\nCross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Mixed-LLM training partially resolves this gap: classification-based DAPT on Qwen+Llama data maintains OOD advantage (DD F1\\,=\\,0.422$\\pm$0.045 vs.\\ vanilla 0.079$\\pm$0.069; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}), though multi-turn human evaluation remains open.\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}\nFITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-turn transfer.}\nToxicChat jailbreaks are exclusively single-turn, while all training data consists of multi-turn conversations (5--14 turns). ToxicChat performance therefore reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer, not standard multi-turn OOD evaluation. The temporal aggregator can learn sequence length as a spurious feature: doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations drops 9-class ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.364$\\pm$0.040, while multi-turn OOD detection remains stable (DD\\,=\\,0.889, ActorAttack\\,=\\,0.913). This length-dependent fragility confirms that the architecture targets multi-turn detection; single-turn robustness requires architectural changes beyond this work's scope.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",
        "numLines": 51,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 51
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/conclusion.tex"
  },

{
  "parentUuid": "3e315f7f-22e7-498a-856d-7ba63ef4d8be",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nThe simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). These failures share a root cause: neither model has been exposed to multi-turn adversarial text. The bottleneck is distributional, not architectural: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nWe test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD}, separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders (125--184M parameters), classification-based domain adaptation is the only universally robust variant. On single-source data, continued pretraining alone closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap; on mixed-LLM data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.422 vs.\\ 0.079 without adaptation). This dependence on adaptation diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation, and three encoder scales together trace a capacity-dependent pattern. Classification supervision also stabilizes predictions on synthetic data regardless of label correctness, though this stability does not transfer to human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the only universally robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels, mean pooling matches BiGRU, and label granularity does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Capacity-dependent pattern for domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) show that the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with model size; RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without any adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} Classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic data, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training validates that classification-based DAPT remains the strongest variant across training compositions (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy enables interpretable strategy characterization (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 24,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 24
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },

{

  "isSidechain": false,

      "type": "text",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization\n\\begin{abstract}\nDetecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families.\nWe isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.\nOn single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.422 vs.\\ 0.079 without adaptation).\nA capacity-dependent pattern holds across scales: as model size grows, the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks, until the largest encoder generalizes without adaptation.\nClassification supervision reduces prediction variance 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic evaluations regardless of label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.\nThese findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 11,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 11
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/abstract.tex"

    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

[Director 指令] [Patch — 最终 placeholder + Appendix 表格]

**最后一个 placeholder**:
- `[EXP17_TOXICCHAT_BALANCED]` = **0.341±0.004**

位置：Limitations Single-turn transfer 段落。注意 exp17 的 std=0.004 说明这是系统性失败——更多训练数据加剧了 GRU 序列长度 spurious feature。

**Appendix 表格**（两个新表）:

**Table: exp16 Mixed Qwen+Llama Training (3 seeds, mean±std)**

| Variant | Mixed Test | DD OOD | AA OOD | ToxicChat† |
|---------|-----------|--------|--------|-----------|
| vanilla | .802±.010 | .079±.069 | .166±.071 | .356±.009 |
| MLM | .681±.226 | .341±.466 | .146±.206 | .256±.053 |
| 9-class | .996±.006 | .422±.045 | .217±.079 | .550±.052 |

† Balanced evaluation (408 samples, 204/204). Zero-shot single-turn transfer; training data contains only multi-turn conversations.

Caption: "Mixed-LLM training results. Classification-based DAPT (9-class) is the only robust variant; MLM collapses with extreme variance."

**Table: exp17 Qwen-only 1000 Scale Validation (3 seeds, mean±std)**

| Variant | DD OOD | AA OOD |
|---------|--------|--------|
| vanilla | .326±.000 | .508±.085 |
| MLM | .338±.010 | .351±.018 |
| 9-class | .889±.041 | .913±.041 |

Note: IID metrics excluded due to data leakage (M019). ToxicChat omitted from main table; see Limitations for single-turn transfer analysis.

Caption: "Dataset scale validation (1,000 conversations). DAPT evidence hierarchy preserved at 2× scale."

**完成后**:
1. 最终 consistency check
2. 确认 0.861 零残留
3. 汇报编译状态
4. 汇报是否可以提交审稿

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0162EitwtsVjsrChqUSymRRn`</summary>

```text
0	% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
1	
2	\section{Discussion}
3	\label{sec:discussion}
4	
5	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
6	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure, not topic-level classification, drives detection.
7	
8	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
9	Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.78--1.00; the residual ActorAttack shortfall (F1\,=\,0.80 vs.\ DD 1.00) reflects cross-LLM distributional differences rather than model failure.
10	Mixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations) reveals a sharper finding: classification-based DAPT is the only robust adaptation across training compositions. On DD OOD, mixed-trained 9-class (F1\,=\,0.422$\pm$0.045) outperforms vanilla (0.079$\pm$0.069) by 34\,pp; MLM collapses with extreme variance (0.341$\pm$0.466). ToxicChat performance shows no significant change between mixed and single-source training (0.550$\pm$0.052 vs.\ 0.596$\pm$0.087). Llama-only training confirms the pattern: MLM $\approx$ vanilla on all OOD sets (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}). Domain exposure closes the OOD gap only when training data shares the target LLM's distributional signature; classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources.
11	
12	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
13	On synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions; MLM's cross-distribution advantage on human data is specific to single-source training conditions.
14	
15	\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}
16	DeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives (RTD vs.\ MLM) and tokenization. The capacity-dependent pattern across three models (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) is suggestive but not conclusive: confirming that model size rather than architecture drives the pattern requires same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\ DeBERTa-large) with matched pretraining.
17	
18	\section{Conclusion}
19	\label{sec:conclusion}
20	
21	Our ablation across three encoder scales, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based DAPT is the only adaptation that robustly bridges the OOD gap across all conditions; unsupervised pretraining closes $\sim$60\,pp on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. For the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) but this benefit reverses on human data. Mixed-LLM training confirms that classification-based DAPT remains the strongest variant across training compositions; multi-turn human evaluation remains future work.
22	
23	\section{Limitations}
24	\label{sec:limitations}
25	
26	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
27	The training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations: classification-based DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization (9-class DD F1\,=\,0.889$\pm$0.041, ActorAttack\,=\,0.913$\pm$0.041), and the hierarchy 9-class $\gg$ MLM $\approx$ vanilla holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\,pp over vanilla). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\,pp over vanilla; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.
28	Cross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Mixed-LLM training partially resolves this gap: classification-based DAPT on Qwen+Llama data maintains OOD advantage (DD F1\,=\,0.422$\pm$0.045 vs.\ vanilla 0.079$\pm$0.069; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}), though multi-turn human evaluation remains open.
29	
30	\paragraph{Lexical separability.}
31	TF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\geq$ 0.89, a $\geq$63\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.
32	
33	\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}
34	Persuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.
35	
36	\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}
37	While the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\% ($\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.
38	
39	\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}
40	FITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.
41	
42	\paragraph{Scope.}
43	Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.
44	
45	\paragraph{Single-turn transfer.}
46	ToxicChat jailbreaks are exclusively single-turn, while all training data consists of multi-turn conversations (5--14 turns). ToxicChat performance therefore reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer, not standard multi-turn OOD evaluation. The temporal aggregator can learn sequence length as a spurious feature: doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations drops 9-class ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.364$\pm$0.040, while multi-turn OOD detection remains stable (DD\,=\,0.889, ActorAttack\,=\,0.913). This length-dependent fragility confirms that the architecture targets multi-turn detection; single-turn robustness requires architectural changes beyond this work's scope.
47	
48	\paragraph{Computation cost.}
49	Stage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.
50	
```

28	\end{table}
29	
30	Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge} reports LLM-as-judge performance on IID and OOD data.
31	On IID Crescendo, GPT-5.5 achieves precision of 1.0 at all turn prefixes but recall ranges from only 0.105 to 0.290, resulting in F1 = 0.383 on full conversations; 30--47\% of responses are unparseable.
32	Qwen3-8B performs worse (IID F1 = 0.100).
33	On OOD data, zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves macro F1 = 0.244 on both DD and ActorAttack by predicting nearly all conversations as benign.
34	Even with 3-shot prompting, Qwen3-8B reaches only 0.566 (DD) and 0.652 (AA), while DisPeD maintains macro F1 $\geq$ 0.972.
35	The poor performance of content-level reasoning on unseen attack types underscores the necessity of trained classifiers with domain-adapted representations.
36

127	Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} shows perturbation behavior on IID data.
128	Classification-supervised variants (9-class, binary, scrambled) achieve zero degradation at full conversation length regardless of label correctness.
129	Models without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\sim$12\,pp.
130	McNemar's test finds no significant pairwise differences among classification-supervised variants (all $p > 0.13$), though with $\leq$7 discordant pairs, statistical power is limited.

154	On OOD DD, the IID pattern replicates: classification-supervised variants show zero degradation while McNemar's test confirms 9-class vs.\ vanilla significance ($p < 0.001$, 49--78 discordant pairs).
155	On OOD ActorAttack, domain-adapted variants degrade substantially (9-class $\Delta$\,=\,$-$20.4$\pm$13.5\,pp, MLM $-$35.3\,pp), but perturbation narrows rather than closes the adapted vs.\ non-adapted gap: 9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\ vanilla 0.248 (McNemar $p < 0.001$).
156	Domain specificity also manifests: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.
157	
158	\section{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}
159	\label{sec:strategy_full}
160	
161	\begin{figure*}[t]
162	\centering
163	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_strategy_heatmap.pdf}
164	\caption{Persuasion strategy transition count matrices for three attack types, based on ground-truth intended strategy labels. Strategy labels validated on $n$=50 samples with a single annotator ($\kappa$=0.78); fine-grained trajectory patterns should be interpreted with this limitation. Crescendo exhibits broad coverage across all strategy categories, while FITD and DD concentrate transitions in smaller subsets. Row $i$, column $j$ counts transitions from strategy $i$ at turn $t$ to strategy $j$ at turn $t+1$.}
165	\label{fig:strategy_heatmap}
166	\end{figure*}
167	
168	Figure~\ref{fig:strategy_heatmap} shows transition count matrices for three attack types.
169	Crescendo conversations exhibit broad transition coverage across all nine strategy categories (1,818 total transitions), with the highest concentration in Role Assignment self-transitions (545); this reflects its gradual escalation design.
170	FITD conversations show a markedly sparser pattern (541 transitions), with Role$\to$Obfuscation (161) as the dominant transition, consistent with FITD's commitment-consistency exploitation~\citep{cialdini2001influence}.
171	DD conversations span 5 active source strategies (565 transitions), with Emotion$\to$Obfuscation (129) as the dominant transition, aligning with DD's design of embedding harmful content within emotionally engaging contexts~\citep{deceptivedelight2024}.
172	
173	\section{Centered CKA Heatmaps}
174	\label{sec:cka_heatmap}
175

Low off-diagonal values (vanilla vs.\ adapted variants) confirm that continued pretraining substantially reshapes representations; the MLM vs.\ 9-class block shows distinct geometries (CKA $\leq$ 0.2 on RoBERTa OOD).}
180	\label{fig:cka_heatmap}
181	\end{figure}

184	\label{app:tsne}

186	Figure~\ref{fig:tsne} visualizes mean-pooled \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via t-SNE for three representative variants (Vanilla, JB-MLM, 9-Class) on DD OOD and ActorAttack. Vanilla embeddings show dispersed jailbreak clusters with ambiguous decision boundaries, while domain-adapted variants (JB-MLM, 9-Class) produce tighter, more separable clusters. JB-MLM achieves the clearest bimodal separation on both evaluation sets, consistent with its superior detection performance.

196	\label{app:cross_llm}
197	
198	\begin{table}[ht]
199	\centering
200	\footnotesize
201	\caption{Cross-LLM validation (F1, full conversation). All models are trained on Qwen3-8B data; conditions vary the source of jailbreak (JB) and benign (ben.) conversations at inference. Values are evaluated under a matched 50-sample subset protocol and differ from Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}, which uses the full evaluation set.}
202	\label{tab:cross_llm}
203	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}
204	\toprule
205	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textit{DD Full}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textit{AA Full}} \\
206	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7}
207	\textbf{Condition} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\
208	\midrule
209	Qwen JB + Qwen ben. & .312 & .914 & .997 & .258 & .893 & .972 \\
210	Llama JB + Llama ben. & .261 & .282 & .433 & .261 & .368 & .321 \\
211	Llama JB + Qwen ben. & .302 & .778$^{*}$ & 1.000 & .302 & .903 & .801 \\
212	\bottomrule

217	
218	Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm} shows three inference conditions. When both jailbreak and benign text come from Llama (row~2), all variants collapse (F1 = 0.26--0.43), but this conflates two sources of distribution shift: unfamiliar jailbreak patterns and unfamiliar benign text. Row~3 isolates jailbreak transferability by pairing Llama jailbreaks with the original Qwen benign distribution. Under this condition, 9-class recovers to F1 = 1.000 on DD and 0.801 on ActorAttack, and JB-MLM reaches 0.903 on ActorAttack. The contrast between rows~2 and~3 shows that the dominant failure mode in row~2 is benign distribution mismatch (false positives from unfamiliar benign text), not inability to recognize cross-LLM jailbreak patterns. Vanilla remains at 0.26--0.31 across all conditions, further confirming that encoders without DAPT lack OOD detection capability regardless of the LLM source. The residual degradation on ActorAttack (0.801 vs.\ 0.972) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation in structurally complex attack patterns.
219	
220	\begin{table}[ht]
221	\centering
222	\footnotesize
223	\caption{Error decomposition for DD Full cross-LLM conditions (matched 50-sample subsets). TP rate is identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse in the Llama-only condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.}
224	\label{tab:cross_llm_error}
225	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
226	\toprule
227	\textbf{Metric} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\
228	\midrule
229	TP rate (Llama JB) & 0.0\% & 68.0\% & 100.0\% \\
230	FP rate (Llama ben.) & 29.3\% & 88.0\% & 90.0\% \\
231	FP rate (Qwen ben.) & 0.0\% & 0.9\% & 0.0\% \\
232	\bottomrule
233	\end{tabular}
234	\end{table}
235	
236	Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error} decomposes DD Full F1 into true positive and false positive rates. TP rates are identical regardless of the benign source: domain-adapted models detect Llama-generated jailbreaks at the same rate whether evaluated against Qwen or Llama benign text. The F1 collapse in the full Llama condition (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (FP rate: 88--90\% on Llama vs.\ 0--0.9\% on Qwen for adapted variants). The model implicitly treats non-Qwen text style as a jailbreak indicator, a spurious correlation from single-LLM training data. This failure reflects a data diversity limitation, not a methodological one; mixed-LLM training (Table~\ref{tab:mixed_llm}) partially addresses this confound.
237	
238	\section{Mixed-LLM Training Results}
239	\label{app:mixed_llm}
240

244	\caption{Mixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations, 3-seed mean$\pm$std where available). ToxicChat evaluated on balanced set (408 conversations). MLM exhibits extreme variance on DD OOD (std\,=\,0.466), driven by one seed predicting all-jailbreak.}
245	\label{tab:mixed_llm}
246	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
247	\toprule
248	\textbf{Variant} & Mixed & DD OOD & AA OOD & ToxicChat \\

251	MLM & .681 & .341$\pm$.466 & .146 & .256$\pm$.053 \\
252	Vanilla & .802 & .079$\pm$.069 & .166 & .356$\pm$.009 \\
253	\bottomrule

255	\end{table}
256	
257	Table~\ref{tab:mixed_llm} reports detection performance when training on a mixed corpus of Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B conversations. Classification-based DAPT (9-class) maintains the strongest performance across all evaluation conditions. MLM continued pretraining collapses on DD OOD with extreme seed variance (std\,=\,0.466), falling below vanilla on ToxicChat (0.256 vs.\ 0.356). ToxicChat performance shows no significant improvement over single-source training (0.550$\pm$0.052 vs.\ 0.596$\pm$0.087; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), consistent with ToxicChat being zero-shot single-turn transfer rather than a standard OOD benchmark.

278	Table~\ref{tab:scale_validation} validates robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations with new topic seeds. The hierarchy 9-class $\gg$ MLM $\approx$ vanilla holds at 2$\times$ scale: classification-based DAPT maintains 40--56\,pp advantage over vanilla, while MLM provides no improvement over vanilla on either attack family (DD: 0.338 vs.\ 0.326; AA: 0.351 vs.\ 0.508).
279	
280	\section{RoBERTa-large DAPT Learning Rate Control}
281	\label{app:roberta_large_lr}
282	
283	\begin{table}[ht]
284	\centering
285	\footnotesize
286	\caption{RoBERTa-large (355M) DAPT learning rate control on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). Reducing the learning rate worsens both DD and AA, ruling out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression in Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}.}
287	\label{tab:roberta_large_lr}
288	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
289	\toprule
290	\textbf{Config} & DD Full & AA Full \\
291	\midrule
292	Vanilla (no DAPT) & .927$\pm$.036 & 1.000$\pm$.000 \\
293	9-class, LR=$2 \times 10^{-5}$ & .987$\pm$.004 & .946$\pm$.057 \\
294	9-class, LR=$1 \times 10^{-5}$ & .948$\pm$.034 & .781$\pm$.054 \\
295	\bottomrule

297	\end{table}
298	
299	Reducing the DAPT learning rate from $2 \times 10^{-5}$ to $1 \times 10^{-5}$ worsens both DD and AA performance, which rules out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression observed in Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}. An MLM-only variant (without classification) produces the same pattern (AA Full = 0.874$\pm$0.014); representation drift from continued pretraining, not classification-specific bias, is the underlying cause. Domain-adaptive fine-tuning narrows the representation space of a sufficiently large encoder and reduces its inherent OOD generalization ability.
300	
301	\section{Reproducibility Details}
302	\label{sec:reproducibility}
303	
304	\paragraph{Stage-1 training.}
305	All variants use DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) with learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, AdamW optimizer ($\beta_1$=0.9, $\beta_2$=0.999, weight decay 0.01), linear warmup over the first 10\% of steps, batch size 16, and 4 epochs. MLM variants use 15\% random token masking. Training takes approximately 30 minutes per variant on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB).
306	
307	\paragraph{Stage-2 training.}
308	The BiGRU has hidden dimension 256, 2 layers, and dropout 0.3 (bidirectional, so output dimension 512). The MLP classifier has layers $512 \to 256 \to 2$ with ReLU activation. Training uses learning rate $1 \times 10^{-3}$, Adam optimizer, batch size 32, and 20 epochs with early stopping (patience 5) on validation F1. Stage-2 training completes in under 5 minutes per variant.
309	
310	\paragraph{Data generation.}
311	Jailbreak conversations are generated using Qwen3-8B with attack-specific prompt templates. Crescendo conversations follow a 5--8 turn gradual escalation structure. FITD conversations use a 2--3 turn commitment-consistency structure. Deceptive Delight conversations embed harmful content within emotionally engaging 3--5 turn sequences. Benign conversations are generated with topic-anchored prompts across five domains (technology, health, finance, education, general) with matched turn counts.
312	
313	\paragraph{Strategy annotation.}
314	Each user turn is annotated by Qwen3-8B using a structured prompt that provides definitions and two examples for each of the nine strategy categories. The prompt instructs the model to output a single category label. We plan to release all generation and annotation prompts, the full dataset, and training code upon acceptance.
315	
316	\paragraph{Adversarial paraphrasing.}
317	The paraphrased test set is generated by instructing Qwen3-8B to rephrase each user turn while preserving the communicative intent. The resulting paraphrases have mean BERTScore 0.92 and mean lexical overlap 0.20 with the originals. These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.
318

ToxicChat evaluated on balanced set (408 conversations). MLM exhibits extreme variance on DD OOD (std\\,=\\,0.466), driven by one seed predicting all-jailbreak.}\n\\label{tab:mixed_llm}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Mixed & DD OOD & AA OOD & ToxicChat \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{.996$\\pm$.006} & \\textbf{.422$\\pm$.045} & \\textbf{.217$\\pm$.079} & \\textbf{.550$\\pm$.052} \\\\\nMLM & .681 & .341$\\pm$.466 & .146 & .256$\\pm$.053 \\\\\nVanilla & .802 & .079$\\pm$.069 & .166 & .356$\\pm$.009 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm} reports detection performance when training on a mixed corpus of Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B conversations. Classification-based DAPT (9-class) maintains the strongest performance across all evaluation conditions. MLM continued pretraining collapses on DD OOD with extreme seed variance (std\\,=\\,0.466), falling below vanilla on ToxicChat (0.256 vs.\\ 0.356). ToxicChat performance shows no significant improvement over single-source training (0.550$\\pm$0.052 vs.\\ 0.596$\\pm$0.087; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), consistent with ToxicChat being zero-shot single-turn transfer rather than a standard OOD benchmark.\n\n\\section{Dataset Scale Validation}\n\\label{app:scale_validation}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Dataset scale validation (1{,}000 Qwen conversations, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). IID results omitted due to train/test overlap (71\\% of original test set present in expanded training data).}\n\\label{tab:scale_validation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD OOD & AA OOD \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{.889$\\pm$.041} & \\textbf{.913$\\pm$.041} \\\\\nMLM & .338$\\pm$.010 & .351$\\pm$.018 \\\\\nVanilla & .326$\\pm$.000 & .508$\\pm$.085 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scale_validation} validates robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to 1{,}000 conversations with new topic seeds. The hierarchy 9-class $\\gg$ MLM $\\approx$ vanilla holds at 2$\\times$ scale: classification-based DAPT maintains 40--56\\,pp advantage over vanilla, while MLM provides no improvement over vanilla on either attack family (DD: 0.338 vs.\\ 0.326; AA: 0.351 vs.\\ 0.508).\n\n\\section{RoBERTa-large DAPT Learning Rate Control}\n\\label{app:roberta_large_lr}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{RoBERTa-large (355M) DAPT learning rate control on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Reducing the learning rate worsens both DD and AA, ruling out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}.}\n\\label{tab:roberta_large_lr}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & DD Full & AA Full \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla (no DAPT) & .927$\\pm$.036 & 1.000$\\pm$.000 \\\\\n9-class, LR=$2 \\times 10^{-5}$ & .987$\\pm$.004 & .946$\\pm$.057 \\\\\n9-class, LR=$1 \\times 10^{-5}$ & .948$\\pm$.034 & .781$\\pm$.054 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nReducing the DAPT learning rate from $2 \\times 10^{-5}$ to $1 \\times 10^{-5}$ worsens both DD and AA performance, which rules out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression observed in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}. An MLM-only variant (without classification) produces the same pattern (AA Full = 0.874$\\pm$0.014); representation drift from continued pretraining, not classification-specific bias, is the underlying cause. Domain-adaptive fine-tuning narrows the representation space of a sufficiently large encoder and reduces its inherent OOD generalization ability.\n\n\\section{Reproducibility Details}\n\\label{sec:reproducibility}\n\n\\paragraph{Stage-1 training.}\nAll variants use DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) with learning rate $2 \\times 10^{-5}$, AdamW optimizer ($\\beta_1$=0.9, $\\beta_2$=0.999, weight decay 0.01), linear warmup over the first 10\\% of steps, batch size 16, and 4 epochs. MLM variants use 15\\% random token masking. Training takes approximately 30 minutes per variant on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB).\n\n\\paragraph{Stage-2 training.}\nThe BiGRU has hidden dimension 256, 2 layers, and dropout 0.3 (bidirectional, so output dimension 512). The MLP classifier has layers $512 \\to 256 \\to 2$ with ReLU activation. Training uses learning rate $1 \\times 10^{-3}$, Adam optimizer, batch size 32, and 20 epochs with early stopping (patience 5) on validation F1. Stage-2 training completes in under 5 minutes per variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Data generation.}\nJailbreak conversations are generated using Qwen3-8B with attack-specific prompt templates. Crescendo conversations follow a 5--8 turn gradual escalation structure. FITD conversations use a 2--3 turn commitment-consistency structure. Deceptive Delight conversations embed harmful content within emotionally engaging 3--5 turn sequences. Benign conversations are generated with topic-anchored prompts across five domains (technology, health, finance, education, general) with matched turn counts.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation.}\nEach user turn is annotated by Qwen3-8B using a structured prompt that provides definitions and two examples for each of the nine strategy categories. The prompt instructs the model to output a single category label. We plan to release all generation and annotation prompts, the full dataset, and training code upon acceptance.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial paraphrasing.}\nThe paraphrased test set is generated by instructing Qwen3-8B to rephrase each user turn while preserving the communicative intent. The resulting paraphrases have mean BERTScore 0.92 and mean lexical overlap 0.20 with the originals. These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.\n",

      "startLine": 28,
      "totalLines": 318
    }
